=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given three length-N sequences of positive integers: A=(A_1,A_2,\ldots,A_N), B=(B_1,B_2,\ldots,B_N), and C=(C_1,C_2,\ldots,C_N).  
Find the number of pairs of positive integers (x, y) that satisfy the following condition:  

- A_i \times x + B_i \times y < C_i for all 1 \leq i \leq N.  

It can be proved that the number of such pairs of positive integers satisfying the condition is finite.  
You are given T test cases, each of which should be solved.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format. Here, \mathrm{case}_i refers to the i-th test case.
T  
\mathrm{case}_1  
\mathrm{case}_2  
\vdots  
\mathrm{case}_T  

Each test case is given in the following format:
N  
A_1 B_1 C_1  
A_2 B_2 C_2  
\vdots  
A_N B_N C_N

Output

Print T lines. The i-th line (1 \leq i \leq T) should contain the answer for \mathrm{case}_i.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 2 \times 10^5 
- 1 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5 
- 1 \leq A_i, B_i, C_i \leq 10^9 
- The sum of N over all test cases is at most 2 \times 10^5.  
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

2
2
1 1 4
1 2 5
1
1 1 2

Sample Output 1

2
0

In the first test case, there are two valid pairs of integers: (x, y) = (1, 1), (2,1). Thus, the first line should contain 2.  
In the second test case, there are no valid pairs of integers. Thus, the second line should contain 0.

5

Sample Output 2

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

So for previous line i and new line j with A_i/B_i < A_j/B_j, define:
num = d_j * B_i - d_i * B_j
den = A_j * B_i - A_i * B_j > 0
x_b = num / den.
Let's verify if line j is ever lower. If num maybe negative, x_b negative, meaning line j lower already for x>negative, so line i may not be active for positive x. If num maybe > den*Xmax, line j never lower within range.

Now hull algorithm with lines sorted by A/B ascending (slope descending). We need maintain hull lines and breakpoints. Let's define each line as tuple (A,B,d) with d=C-1. For line i, value at x = (d_i - A_i x)/B_i. Sort key: A_i/B_i ascending. For equal slope, keep smallest d/B.

Hull list lines in envelope order (same as sorted). Breakpoints list bp[k] for k>=1: the real x where hull[k] becomes better than hull[k-1] (i.e., intersection). We can store as rational (num, den) with den>0. For first line, no bp.

  If hull empty: append L.
  Else:
    While hull not empty:

Now, floor_sum implementation. AtCoder floor_sum:
def floor_sum(n, m, a, b):
    # sum_{0<=i<n} floor((a*i+b)/m)
    ans=0
    while True:
        if a>=m:
            ans += (n-1)*n*(a//m)//2
            a %= m
        if b>=m:
            ans += n*(b//m)
            b %= m
        y_max = a*n + b
        if y_max < m:
            break
        n = y_max // m
        b = y_max % m
        m, a = a, m
    return ans
Need ensure a,b nonnegative. In our call a=A (positive), b=d - A*r. Since r<=Xmax, b>=B? Actually d - A*r >= B? Wait for line i, r<=Xmax<=floor((d_i-B_i)/A_i), so d_i - A_i*r >= B_i. So b>=B_i >0. Good. n = r-l+1 positive. m=B_i positive. a=A_i positive. Good. floor_sum returns sum over t=0..n-1 floor((A*t + b)/B). This equals sum_{x=l}^r floor((d - A*(r-t))/B) = floor((d - A*r + A*t)/B). Yes.

But wait if we use line i that is minimal over [l,r], f(x)=floor((d_i - A_i x)/B_i). But if multiple lines equal at some x, using any gives same floor because equal rational => same floor. Good.

Let's detail hull algorithm with rational breakpoints.

Data structures:
line = (A, B, d) maybe also original index? Not needed.
sort lines by A/B ascending. Use cmp_to_key:
def cmp(l1,l2):
    # compare A1/B1 < A2/B2
    lhs = l1[0]*l2[1]
    rhs = l2[0]*l1[1]
    if lhs != rhs:
        return -1 if lhs < rhs else 1
    # same slope, compare d1/B1 < d2/B2
    lhs = l1[2]*l2[1]
    rhs = l2[2]*l1[1]

Comparator with cross multiplication:

        return -1 if c < 0 else 1  # d1/B1 < d2/B2
    return 0

Now, let's verify floor_sum implementation thoroughly.

Standard AtCoder floor_sum:
def floor_sum(n, m, a, b):
    ans = 0
    while True:
        if a >= m:
            ans += (n - 1) * n * (a // m) // 2
            a %= m
        if b >= m:
            ans += n * (b // m)
            b %= m
        y_max = a * n + b
        if y_max < m:
            break
        n = y_max // m
        b = y_max % m
        m, a = a, m
    return ans
This assumes 0 <= a,b? It works for nonnegative. After reductions, a,b nonnegative. In loop, after m,a swap, a could be 0? If a=0, then y_max=b < m after b reduction? If a=0, y_max=b<m, break. If m becomes 0? If a=0, we don't swap because break before. If a>0, m becomes a >0. Good.

Let's test floor_sum with m=1. If a>=m, ans += (n-1)*n*(a//1)//2, a%=1 =>0. b>=1, ans += n*b, b%=1 =>0. y_max=0*n+0=0 <1 break. Sum floor((a*i+b)/1) = a*n(n-1)/2 + b*n. Correct. In our call m=B could be 1. Good.

Now, in our floor_sum call, a=A_i, b=d - A_i*R. But if A_i is large and R large, b could be as low as B_i (positive). If B_i=1, b>=1. Good.

Now, let's think about if the line segment [L,R] includes x where line value is not minimal due to floor? We rely on real envelope. But what if at integer x, two lines cross between integers; the lower real line changes at floor(x_b)+1. We use that. Good.

Now, let's consider if the lower envelope of rational lines has breakpoints that are not monotonic due to sorting? The hull algorithm ensures. But let's prove with our sort A/B ascending. For two lines i,j with A_i/B_i < A_j/B_j, line i is lower for x < x_ij, line j lower for x > x_ij. Thus as x increases, A/B of optimal line increases. So envelope order is sorted. Good.

Now, let's think about the possibility of vertical asymptote? No.

Now, one important issue: We are summing f(x) = min_i floor((d_i - A_i x)/B_i). But the lower envelope of real lines g(x)=min_i (d_i - A_i x)/B_i is concave. The floor of a concave function is not necessarily concave, but that's okay. Our segment sum uses real envelope.

Now, let's consider if the answer can be large. Number of pairs finite but could be up to ~1e18? x up to 1e9, y up to 1e9, sum ~1e18. Python int fine. Output as int.

Now, let's think if there is a simpler formula using Pick's theorem that might be expected? But our approach is solid.

Need ensure we don't miss constraints with y lower bound? We count y>=1 by f(x) floor value. Since f(x)>=1 for x<=Xmax. Good.

Now, let's consider potential bug in hull algorithm: We compute intersection x = (d_new * B_last - d_last * B_new) / (A_new * B_last - A_last * B_new). Den positive because A_new/B_new > A_last/B_last. But what if A_new/B_new is greater but B_last or B_new large, den positive. Good.

Let's derive formula again carefully to avoid sign error.

Line last: y_last = (d_last - A_last x) / B_last.
Line new: y_new = (d_new - A_new x) / B_new.
Set equal:
(d_last - A_last x) B_new = (d_new - A_new x) B_last
d_last B_new - A_last B_new x = d_new B_last - A_new B_last x
Bring x terms:
A_new B_last x - A_last B_new x = d_new B_last - d_last B_new
x (A_new B_last - A_last B_new) = d_new B_last - d_last B_new
So num = d_new * B_last - d_last * B_new. Den = A_new * B_last - A_last * B_new. Yes. Example l1 d=5,A=1,B=1; l2 d=10,A=2,B=1: num=10*1-5*1=5, den=2*1-1*1=1, x=5. Good.

Now, compare x <= bp[-1]. bp[-1] is start of last line, i.e., intersection of previous line and last line. For previous p, last l, new n:
bp_last = (d_last*B_prev - d_prev*B_last) / (A_last*B_prev - A_prev*B_last). Den positive because A_last/B_last > A_prev/B_prev. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a known AtCoder problem? Maybe "AtCoder ABC 283 Ex?" Not sure. The solution might involve "min-plus convolution"? But our approach likely.

Let's scrutinize the mathematical reduction more.

Original inequalities:
A_i x + B_i y < C_i.
For fixed integer x>=1, y positive integer. Let d_i = C_i -1.
Then y <= floor((d_i - A_i x)/B_i). Let U(x) = min_i floor(...). Count = sum_{x=1}^{Xmax} U(x), where Xmax = max x with U(x)>=1. Since U(x) nonincreasing? Is U(x) nonincreasing? Each floor line is nonincreasing, min of nonincreasing is nonincreasing. So U(x)>=1 for x<=Xmax and U(x)<1 for x>Xmax. Xmax = max{x | U(x)>=1}. We computed as min_i floor((d_i - B_i)/A_i). Is that equal? U(x)>=1 iff for all i, floor((d_i - A_i x)/B_i) >=1 iff for all i, d_i - A_i x >= B_i iff x <= (d_i - B_i)/A_i. Since x integer, x <= floor((d_i - B_i)/A_i). Thus max x = min_i floor(...). Yes.

Now, U(x) = min_i floor(L_i(x)) = floor(min_i L_i(x))? Let's prove: For any set of real numbers r_i, min_i floor(r_i) = floor(min_i r_i). Let r_min = min_i r_i. For all i, r_min <= r_i, so floor(r_min) <= floor(r_i), so floor(r_min) <= min_i floor(r_i). Also let j be index with r_j = r_min. Then min_i floor(r_i) <= floor(r_j) = floor(r_min). Thus equality. Good. So U(x) = floor(G(x)) where G(x)=min_i (d_i - A_i x)/B_i. Great.

Now, G(x) is lower envelope of lines. We build hull.

Now, let's think about if the lower envelope of lines over all real x is exactly what hull algorithm computes. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with lines that are identical but different A/B? Identical line means same value for all x, so A/B and d/B same. Reduction handles.

Now, let's think about if the hull algorithm's breakpoints are stored as rational but we only need floor. Could we store floor directly and compare using floor? No, need exact for popping. We store rational.

Now, let's consider if the breakpoint formula's numerator and denominator can be simplified? Not necessary.

Now, let's think about if floor_sum can be optimized by using `while True` and no recursion. Good.

Now, let's test floor_sum with a case where a=0? In our call A_i>=1, so a>0. But after swaps, a can become 0. Standard handles.

Now, let's think about if `b = d - A * R` could be negative if R > Xmax? We set R<=Xmax. Good. But what if Xmax computed original and line is not original? It is original. Good.

Now, let's consider if the hull algorithm's unique lines after reduction might have a line with d - A*Xmax < B? No, because Xmax min over original all lines, so for every original line, d - A*Xmax >= B. Unique subset original. Good.

Now, let's think about if the answer for sample 1 first test: We got 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with using `d = C - 1` and `C` up to 1e9, so d up to 999,999,999. Cross products d*B up to 1e18, okay.

Now, let's consider if the problem might have multiple test cases with sum N 2e5 but T 2e5; if we early return for Xmax<1, we should still consume input. We parse all data first, so okay.

Now, let's think about if we need to reset lists per test. Yes.

Now, let's write the code structure:

import sys
from functools import cmp_to_key

def floor_sum(n, m, a, b):
    ans = 0
    while True:
        if a >= m:
            ans += (n - 1) * n * (a // m) // 2
            a %= m
        if b >= m:
            ans += n * (b // m)
            b %= m
        y_max = a * n + b
        if y_max < m:
            break
        n = y_max // m
        b = y_max % m
        m, a = a, m
    return ans

def cmp_slope(p, q):
    A1,B1,_ = p
    A2,B2,_ = q
    c = A1*B2 - A2*B1
    if c < 0:
        return -1
    if c > 0:
        return 1
    return 0

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = next(it)
        lines = []
        Xmax = 10**30? Actually need min. Initialize large.
        Xmax = 10**30
        for _ in range(N):
            A = next(it); B = next(it); C = next(it)
            d = C - 1
            lines.append((A,B,d))
            # Xmax = min(Xmax, (d - B)//A)
            val = (d - B) // A
            if val < Xmax:
                Xmax = val
        if Xmax < 1:
            out.append("0")
            continue
        # sort lines by slope
        lines.sort(key=cmp_to_key(cmp_slope))
        # reduce same slope
        unique = []
        i = 0
        Ln = len(lines)
        while i < Ln:
            A0,B0,d0 = lines[i]
            best = lines[i]
            j = i + 1
            while j < Ln and lines[j][0] * B0 == A0 * lines[j][1]:
                A,B,d = lines[j]
                # compare d/B with best's d/B
                if d * best[1] < best[2] * B:
                    best = (A,B,d)
                j += 1
            unique.append(best)
            i = j
        # build hull
        hull = []
        bp = []  # list of (num, den)
        for A,B,d in unique:
            while True:
                if not hull:
                    hull.append((A,B,d))
                    break
                lastA,lastB,lastD = hull[-1]
                # den should be >0
                den = A * lastB - lastA * B
                if den <= 0:
                    # same slope or sorting issue; handle safely
                    # If new line is not worse, replace last
                    num = d * lastB - lastD * B
                    if num <= 0:
                        hull.pop()
                        if bp:
                            bp.pop()
                        continue
                    else:
                        # new line is dominated
                        break
                num = d * lastB - lastD * B
                if bp:
                    start_num, start_den = bp[-1]
                    # if num/den <= start_num/start_den
                    if num * start_den <= start_num * den:
                        hull.pop()
                        bp.pop()
                        continue
                hull.append((A,B,d))
                bp.append((num, den))
                break
        # sum
        ans = 0
        M = len(hull)
        for idx, (A,B,d) in enumerate(hull):
            if idx == 0:
                L = 1
            else:
                start_num, start_den = bp[idx-1]
                L = start_num // start_den + 1
                if L < 1:
                    L = 1
            if idx == M - 1:
                R = Xmax
            else:
                next_num, next_den = bp[idx]
                R = next_num // next_den
                if R > Xmax:
                    R = Xmax
            if L <= R:
                n = R - L + 1
                b = d - A * R
                ans += floor_sum(n, B, A, b)
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()

if den == 0:
    # same slope; should not happen after reduction
    # compare d/B
    num = d * lastB - lastD * B
    if num <= 0:
        hull.pop()
        if bp: bp.pop()

        break
if den < 0:
    # Should not happen; but if it does, the order is wrong. We can still compute?

    # We could swap? But not needed.
    pass

Wait, this is important! Let's examine.

We assumed envelope order by A/B ascending: smaller A/B (less negative slope) first at small x, larger A/B later at large x. But what if a line with larger A/B has much smaller intercept, so it is lower at x=0? Then it would be optimal at small x, contradicting order. Let's test.

Line1: A=1,B=1,d=100 => y=100-x (slope -1, intercept 100)
Line2: A=2,B=1,d=10 => y=10-2x (slope -2, intercept 10)
A/B: line1=1, line2=2. Sorted ascending: line1, line2. At x=0, line2=10 < line1=100, so line2 is lower at small x. Intersection: 100-x = 10-2x => x = -90. For x > -90, line2 is lower. So line2 is optimal for all positive x. Envelope order should be line2 then line1? But line1 is never optimal for positive x. The hull algorithm with sorted line1,line2 appends line2 with bp=-90. The envelope order in hull is [line1, line2] with line1 active x<-90, line2 active x>-90. For positive x, line2 is optimal. So the order by A/B ascending is still the order of optimality as x increases, but the first line may have no positive domain. The line with larger A/B can be optimal at small x if its intercept is much smaller; then the intersection is negative, and the smaller A/B line is only optimal for x < negative, i.e., not in positive domain. Thus the envelope order over all real x is still line1 (small A/B) for x < -90, line2 (large A/B) for x > -90. As x increases, A/B increases. So sorted ascending is correct. Good.

Now, consider line1: A=1,B=1,d=10 (y=10-x), line2: A=2,B=1,d=100 (y=100-2x). Intersection x=90. line1 optimal x<90, line2 x>90. Sorted line1,line2. Good.
Consider line1: A=1,B=1,d=10, line2: A=2,B=1,d=5. Intersection x=-5. line2 optimal x>-5, so line2 for positive. Sorted line1,line2. Good.
So envelope order by A/B ascending always holds over real x, even if some initial lines have no positive domain. Because for any two lines, the one with smaller A/B is lower to the left of their intersection, larger A/B lower to the right. Thus as x increases, optimal A/B nondecreasing. Good.

Let's maybe write the final code cleanly:

import sys
from functools import cmp_to_key

def floor_sum(n, m, a, b):
    ans = 0
    while True:
        if a >= m:
            ans += (n - 1) * n * (a // m) // 2
            a %= m
        if b >= m:
            ans += n * (b // m)
            b %= m
        y_max = a * n + b
        if y_max < m:
            break
        n = y_max // m
        b = y_max % m
        m, a = a, m
    return ans

def cmp_slope(p, q):
    v = p[0] * q[1] - q[0] * p[1]
    if v < 0:
        return -1
    if v > 0:
        return 1
    return 0

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = next(it)
        lines = []
        Xmax = 10**30
        for _ in range(N):
            A = next(it); B = next(it); C = next(it)
            d = C - 1
            lines.append((A, B, d))
            val = (d - B) // A
            if val < Xmax:
                Xmax = val
        if Xmax < 1:
            out.append("0")
            continue

        lines.sort(key=cmp_to_key(cmp_slope))

        unique = []
        i = 0
        Ln = len(lines)
        while i < Ln:
            A0, B0, d0 = lines[i]
            best = lines[i]
            j = i + 1
            while j < Ln and lines[j][0] * B0 == A0 * lines[j][1]:
                A, B, d = lines[j]
                if d * best[1] < best[2] * B:
                    best = (A, B, d)
                j += 1
            unique.append(best)
            i = j

        hull = []
        bp = []
        for A, B, d in unique:
            while True:
                if not hull:
                    hull.append((A, B, d))
                    break
                lastA, lastB, lastD = hull[-1]
                den = A * lastB - lastA * B
                num = d * lastB - lastD * B
                if den == 0:
                    # same slope (should not happen after reduction)
                    if num <= 0:
                        hull.pop()
                        if bp:
                            bp.pop()
                        continue
                    else:
                        break
                if bp:
                    s_num, s_den = bp[-1]
                    if num * s_den <= s_num * den:
                        hull.pop()
                        bp.pop()
                        continue
                hull.append((A, B, d))
                bp.append((num, den))
                break

        ans = 0
        M = len(hull)
        for idx, (A, B, d) in enumerate(hull):
            if idx == 0:
                L = 1
            else:
                s_num, s_den = bp[idx - 1]
                L = s_num // s_den + 1
                if L < 1:
                    L = 1
            if L > Xmax:
                break
            if idx == M - 1:
                R = Xmax
            else:
                n_num, n_den = bp[idx]
                R = n_num // n_den
                if R > Xmax:
                    R = Xmax
            if L <= R:
                n = R - L + 1
                b = d - A * R
                ans += floor_sum(n, B, A, b)
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()

Need check the `den == 0` branch: If den==0 and num<=0, we pop last. But what if hull has previous lines and bp not empty, we pop bp. Good. If den==0 and num>0, we `break` out of while, skipping new line. But if we break, the for loop continues to next unique line. Good. However, if den==0 and num>0, new line is dominated by last. But what if last line is later popped by a subsequent line? Then the skipped new line might become relevant? If new line is dominated by last (same slope, larger intercept), and last is popped by a subsequent line with even smaller value, then new line is also dominated by that subsequent line? Since new line has same slope as last and larger intercept, if subsequent line dominates last at some x, it also dominates new? Need check: same slope, new line > last for all x. If subsequent line is lower than last for some interval, it is also lower than new. So new line never needed. Safe.

But if den==0 and num<=0, new line dominates last. We pop last and continue. If hull becomes empty, append new. If not, recompute with previous. Good.

Now, let's think about if `den` could be negative in the `den == 0` branch? We only check `==0`, so negative would proceed to use den negative in comparison and floor division, causing wrong. But den negative impossible. To be absolutely safe, we could handle `if den <= 0:` and if den<0, maybe swap? But if den<0, sorting order is violated; perhaps due to equal? Let's not overcomplicate. Since sort and reduction guarantee den>0. We can omit den==0 branch entirely for speed and clarity. But if den==0 due to same slope, division by zero. Reduction prevents. I think it's safe to omit. However, including `if den == 0` doesn't hurt. But if den<0, it won't catch. Could use `if den <= 0:` and if den<0, maybe treat as same slope? That would be wrong but den<0 impossible. I'll use `if den == 0` for safety against same slope only. But den<0 impossible, so okay.

=== ANSWER ===
got cut off due to length but I think enough. Need final code.

Let's ensure final code is syntactically correct.

Potential issue: In hull algorithm, if `den == 0` and `num > 0`, we `break` out of while True, skipping the new line. But if `den == 0` and `num <= 0`, we pop last and continue. However, if `den == 0` and `num > 0`, new line dominated by last. But what if last line is not in hull? It is. Good.

But wait: If `den == 0` and `num > 0`, new line has same slope and larger intercept than last. It is dominated by last. But what if last line is later popped by a subsequent line, and new line might be needed? We argued no. Good.

Now, let's think about if `den == 0` branch could be removed to avoid potential infinite loop? No.

Now, final code:

```python
import sys
from functools import cmp_to_key

def floor_sum(n, m, a, b):
    ans = 0
    while True:
        if a >= m:
            ans += (n - 1) * n * (a // m) // 2
            a %= m
        if b >= m:
            ans += n * (b // m)
            b %= m
        y_max = a * n + b
        if y_max < m:
            break
        n = y_max // m
        b = y_max % m
        m, a = a, m
    return ans

def cmp_slope(p, q):
    v = p[0] * q[1] - q[0] * p[1]
    if v < 0:
        return -1
    if v > 0:
        return 1
    return 0

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = next(it)
        lines = []
        Xmax = 10**30
        for _ in range(N):
            A = next(it); B = next(it); C = next(it)
            d = C - 1
            lines.append((A, B, d))
            val = (d - B) // A
            if val < Xmax:
                Xmax = val
        if Xmax < 1:
            out.append("0")
            continue

        lines.sort(key=cmp_to_key(cmp_slope))

        unique = []
        i = 0
        Ln = len(lines)
        while i < Ln:
            A0, B0, d0 = lines[i]
            best = lines[i]
            j = i + 1
            while j < Ln and lines[j][0] * B0 == A0 * lines[j][1]:
                A, B, d = lines[j]
                if d * best[1] < best[2] * B:
                    best = (A, B, d)
                j += 1
            unique.append(best)
            i = j

        hull = []
        bp = []
        for A, B, d in unique:
            while True:
                if not hull:
                    hull.append((A, B, d))
                    break
                lastA, lastB, lastD = hull[-1]
                den = A * lastB - lastA * B
                num = d * lastB - lastD * B
                if den == 0:
                    if num <= 0:
                        hull.pop()
                        if bp:
                            bp.pop()
                        continue
                    else:
                        break
                if bp:
                    s_num, s_den = bp[-1]
                    if num * s_den <= s_num * den:
                        hull.pop()
                        bp.pop()
                        continue
                hull.append((A, B, d))
                bp.append((num, den))
                break

        ans = 0
        M = len(hull)
        for idx, (A, B, d) in enumerate(hull):
            if idx == 0:
                L = 1
            else:
                s_num, s_den = bp[idx - 1]
                L = s_num // s_den + 1
                if L < 1:
                    L = 1
            if L > Xmax:
                break
            if idx == M - 1:
                R = Xmax
            else:
                n_num, n_den = bp[idx]
                R = n_num // n_den
                if R > Xmax:
                    R = Xmax
            if L <= R:
                n = R - L + 1
                b = d - A * R
                ans += floor_sum(n, B, A, b)
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

Need check if `d0` unused in reduction; okay. Could remove but fine.

Now, let's think about if `Xmax` can be 0 and we skip sorting; but we already read lines. Good.

Now, let's consider if the `den == 0` branch could cause a line to be skipped (break) but if that line has same slope and larger intercept, it is dominated. Good.

Now, let's think about if `den == 0` and `num <= 0`, we pop last. But if `num == 0`, new line identical to last; popping last and appending new is okay. If `num < 0`, new lower. Good.

Now, let's consider if `den` could be negative due to sorting bug; our code would not catch and would use negative den in comparison and floor division, causing wrong. But sorting by cmp_slope ensures den>0. If same slope, den=0. So no negative. Good.

Now, let's think about if the `cmp_slope` comparator returns 0 for equal slopes, but if p and q have same slope and different d/B, sort stable. Good.

Now, let's consider if the reduction loop's `best` comparison uses `d * best[1] < best[2] * B`. If best is updated, best[1],best[2] from new best. Good.

Now, let's think about if the `unique` list after reduction preserves sorted order by A/B. Yes.

Now, let's consider if the hull algorithm's `bp` list after processing all unique lines has length M-1. Yes.

Now, let's think about if the sum loop's `if L > Xmax: break` could break before processing a line that has L>Xmax but previous line's R was less than Xmax due to floor(bp) < Xmax? We reasoned impossible. Let's double-check with bp integer: previous R = floor(bp) = bp. current L = bp+1. If L>Xmax => bp+1>Xmax => bp>=Xmax. previous R = min(Xmax, bp) = Xmax. So previous covered to Xmax. If bp non-integer: previous R=floor(bp), current L=floor(bp)+1. If L>Xmax => floor(bp)+1>Xmax => floor(bp)>=Xmax. previous R=min(Xmax, floor(bp))=Xmax. Good. So break safe.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code's `floor_sum` if `n` is large and `a` is 0 after modulo, break. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the code's `out` if T large and no newline at end; accepted.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code's `floor_sum` if `m` is 0? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the code's `if den == 0` branch and `num > 0` break; if we break, we skip new line, but if new line is dominated by last, and last is later popped by a subsequent line, new line might not be dominated by subsequent? We argued yes dominated. Let's prove with same slope: last and new have same slope m, new intercept > last intercept. If subsequent line s is lower than last for some interval, then s < last < new, so new not needed. If s pops last, s <= last everywhere, so s < new. Safe.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code's `unique` reduction if same slope group has lines with same d/B but different A,B and one has smaller A? They are identical value, but floor_sum with different A,B? Wait if A/B same and d/B same, then (d - A x)/B = d/B - (A/B)x. This depends only on d/B and A/B, not on individual A,B. So any tuple yields same rational value. But floor_sum with A,B from tuple computes floor((d - A x)/B) = floor(d/B - (A/B)x), same. Good.
